#6776. “Two Girls Who Speak All the Time About Politics”: Tao Okamoto and Virginie Efira Take On Capitalism in All of a Sudden

Okamoto and Efira shared the Best Actress Award at Cannes, where All of a Sudden premiered this summer

We’re all seeking comfort as the world ends. Environmental, economic, and political pressures weigh heavily on our minds as tangible solutions feel farther and farther out of reach. Solace is a rare commodity in a world controlled by unrelenting capitalist forces. In All of a Sudden, filmmaker Ryusuke Hamaguchi suggests that as we face tragedy, we must remember our vital need for human connection.

All of a Sudden tells the story of two women who forge a formidable friendship as they face personal and professional hardship. Marie-Lou Fontaine (Virginie Efira) runs an elder-care facility near Paris, where she introduces a “Humanitude” methodology designed to treat patients with dignity and introduce listening-heavy caretaking practice. She befriends Mari (Tao Okamoto), a terminally ill theater director from Japan. Ryusuke chronicles this relationship through extended, emotional conversations, spoken in both Japanese and French, and covering topics from identity to mortality.

The film is an adaptation of a nonfiction book, When Life Suddenly Takes a Turn: Twenty Letters Between a Philosopher With Terminal Cancer and a Medical Anthropologist, which collects correspondence from two Japanese-speaking academics as they reflect on health care and death during the last few months of philosopher Makiko Miyano’s life (who inspired Okamoto’s character). Instead of keeping the fact that the two pen pals spoke the same language, he introduced French and moved the story to Paris. This small change forced the story to confront language gaps, exploring the power of conversation and listening and asserting that so much can be achieved through this mediation.

Efira and Okamoto understand their characters’ simmering anxieties and outlooks on the world, providing the bedrock for these impressively naturalistic performances. The two actresses sustain long, philosophical conversation with such intensity throughout the entire three hours and 16 minutes. In a rare occurrence, the two shared the Best Actress Award at the Cannes Film Festival, where the movie premiered this summer. In the film, their budding friendship serves as a sounding board for each other, as Marie-Lou grapples with the pressure of capitalism on her medical staff and Mari comes to terms with the end of her life. In typical fashion, Ryusuke lets the camera linger on these two actresses during these prolonged philosophical interactions, forcing the audience to sit with the gravity of their situations.

Ahead of the theatrical release of Ryusuke’s latest feature film, Bazaar spoke to Efira and Okamoto about the first time they read the script, how they built an intimate bond despite speaking two different languages, and the punk, anti-capitalist edge fueling this intimate, conversation-heavy film.

Tao Okamoto: It’s the scene where she talks about capitalism while writing on the whiteboard. That’s one of the strangest scenes in the movie. I felt connected to the character because I am also a climate activist myself. I’ve been spreading the word about climate crises on podcasts, and I talk about it daily. When I read the part, I felt, “I need to play this character.”

Virginie Efira: It’s the same for me, because this scene is so special. When I read it, the movie felt like one of the most profound, special, and radical readings. You have two girls who speak all the time about politics. We don’t see that. I never see that in other movies. Sometimes, when a director wants to put something philosophical, political, or intellectual, they say, “Maybe we’ll do three minutes,” otherwise the audience will be bored. No, this movie spent 15 minutes [discussing capitalism], and I said, “Oh yes, that’s so punk!” The film has its own language. When I read All of a Sudden, I could hear the language of the movie, and I wanted to approach it. When I met Tao, I thought, “Oh, we are so lucky to be an actress and work with [Ryusuke], but also to share this material with him.”

VE: I only knew one word in Japanese before making the movie. Then, to be able to speak Japanese at a high level, because the character studied in Japan, I must first learn to read, then learn to embody the language. Finding the language of the movie was strange. There’s a flat and natural way to play [in one of Ryusuke’s movies]. He spoke to me about Cassavetes and Gena Rowlands, but also about Bresson. Neutral performances. You find it when you open up, and when I opened to Tao, we discovered the right language for the movie. We did a lot of flat readings before shooting. We never rehearsed in motion, so when we finally performed for the first time, we awakened the text.

TO: I’m going to add that because I started my career in America, finding a middle ground by using a language that’s not my mother tongue is normal for me. On the surface, that part wasn’t a challenge for me. Finding the language for this film and respecting Hamaguchi’s way of working were priorities. We were naturally drawn to his talent and wanted to make his dream happen and be part of it.

TO: I joined this project way after Virginie. I luckily had months to prepare with him, and during rehearsals, you drop all emotion and read the text as if you were reading numbers. On top of that, I got to visit some institutes, such as the Cancer Research Center and places where people work with disabled people, to build up my understanding of my role’s point of view. Reading the text over and over was a unique experience for me. [Ryusuke] can really listen to the actor’s voice and tell the difference between someone who is simply reading and someone really concentrating. Even though you’re not supposed to put any emotions, you have to be present; you have to be there. I’ve never experienced such rich, profound preparation for a role.

VE: We shot in a real nursing home. There was a mix between fiction and reality. Sometimes filmmaking becomes standardized, with no room left to breathe, but with Hamaguchi, you’re sure he will leave room for the unexpected and for uncertainty. It allows you to embrace the unpredictability of reality. He’s not looking for an accident, but for the ability to be in the present. A viewer of Hamaguchi’s work will always feel a sensation of documentation, but it’s still always cinema. To be an actor in his movies is to be naturalistic, to be in the present, to capture some details and really embrace this unpredictability. The filming process becomes an adventure he wants you to have. One day, he told me, “I hope that you will find the light.” He said a lot of cryptic things. He said to me, “Don’t use your text like a tool; just go inside the text.”

TO: The script is already amazing. And I could read it over and over again, and that was a joy. But he mentioned that I should read the original book. It’s about two women exchanging letters—or emails. They are real people who the characters are modeled after. I wanted to please the people who know these women, not to make them too different from them. I first put pressure on myself to portray this woman as close to the real person, but it was impossible for me because this person passed away in 2019. I couldn’t do much research, so I interviewed her mother and the co-writer. That provided me with a lot of clues, but reading their exchanged letters already gave me the soul of this movie.

VE: He gives the book to the actresses, but also to the entire team. I never felt something so collective, and you will feel it in the movie, from the sound engineer to someone who only stayed two days on set. I also met the anthropologist. When you read this book, it’s full of life. It gives you a perceptible beauty of things. The book has a kind of energy; it helps you keep a level of optimism even when you know the situation is hopeless. In this book, it speaks about what it’s like to be alive. Maybe there’s beauty in life because we are going to die.

TO: For me, I came on this project at the last minute, only a month before. Virginie was so dedicated to studying Japanese from scratch; it was so impressive how she studied all this writing, reading, and lines. Seeing that, I had a huge admiration, like “Who can do this!?” As you can see, Virginie’s so charming; nobody would hate her. It was so easy for me to be drawn to her as a person, as the characters in the movie were discovering each other.

VE: We got to know each other through the shoot. I was happy not to know much at the beginning and to progressively get deeper into our relationship. We lived all that together. I conflate Marie-Lou’s feelings for Mari with Virginie’s feelings for Tao, because we share that. It will be an end, not a tragic one like in the movie, but it will be an end. But I know Tao will carry this experience in her heart forever, and I will too. He let us feel what we want to feel.

TO: I study environmental issues, and I always thought capitalism is responsible for our world’s issues. Something I didn’t know until I read this script was that capitalism doesn’t only exploit our environment, but also our human bodies—human labor and our time. When I connected those dots, I realized all the problems could be blamed on capitalism. We’re looking for time and looking for happiness—or security—and all these things can be related to a capitalistic way of living. I think it’s great that Hamaguchi connected that fundamental issue to Marie-Lou’s situation in the elderly home. She’s frustrated, as is Marie. She’s battling her cancer, and it can be connected to this economic issue.

VE: Cinema can show bodies, fatigue, and with Marie-Lou, you see a certain fatigue because capitalism creates it. How do we deal with that? We say, “Oh, capitalism’s not good. We don’t have time. And we want to be richer and richer.” But how do we fight that, because everything is already there? I come from France, and you see all the young people outside. The real question is, what can cinema do with that? It’s not a theoretical thing, but Hamaguchi can find a solution. It’s speaking, listening to the other, sharing ideas; it’s movement. If I look for a solution to this problem with money, I’ll remain a prisoner of this way of functioning. Even if you think you’re not in a stage of capitalism, you are.

This interview has been condensed and edited for clarity.

#6771. Jennifer Lawrence Sets the Record Straight on Those Blepharoplasty Rumors

The actress credits her makeup artist, Shayna Gold, as the mastermind behind her “bleph”

In this day and age, it’s almost a rite of passage for a celebrity to have plastic surgery rumors about them swirling online, but Jennifer Lawrence has been speaking her truth on the matter as of late.

Last week on Watch What Happens Live, the Hunger Games actress casually slipped into her conversation with host Andy Cohen that she has new breast implants and even credited her plastic surgeon, Steven Teitelbaum, for the “full C” cups.

She continues to set the record straight on her cosmetic procedures—this time shutting down rumors that she had a blepharoplasty. Online rumors quickly speculated that Lawrence had undergone upper eyelid surgery, but her latest Instagram post shuts them down. Her makeup artist, Shayna Gold, shared a mailer of products from the actress’s new haircare line, Mes, and Lawrence reposted the photo to her own story with a cheeky caption. “Iykyk she’s the one who did my bleph,” Lawrence wrote, crediting Gold and pointing to makeup as the reason she looks so wide awake, officially poking holes in any eyelid surgery rumors.

Lawrence has previously been open about her plastic surgery. In October, after gracing the cover of The Cut, she revealed she had work done following the birth of her second child, which the publication then specified as a boob job. One year earlier, the actress told The New Yorker, “Everything bounced back, pretty much, after the first one. Second one, nothing bounced back.”

While fans aren’t exactly sold on Lawrence’s denial of a bleph, makeup artists often do have tricks up their sleeves for faking an eyelid lift. For starters, placement matters. When emphasizing the crease, apply shadow above the natural crease. “I like patting shadows onto the lid first, then blending the edges after. Too much back-and-forth blending can make the shape disappear,” celebrity makeup artist Christian Briceno previously told Bazaar. Also, opt for an elongated look, like a soft wing, to make the eye look instantly bigger. This also applies to mascara—artists recommend focusing application on the outer corners versus the center.

#6770. Your Vagina Has a Party Trick, and It's Called Pompoir

Meet the sex technique that puts your pelvic floor muscles to work.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

If you have a vagina (or happen to be particularly fond of them), there's a decent chance you've heard of Kegels. You know, those pelvic floor exercises that involve squeezing and releasing muscles you might not think about all that often, but the second someone mentions them, you immediately start clenching (like right now).

But what if you could put those same muscles to work during sex? That's where pompoir comes in, using different rhythms and contractions to create new sensations during penetration, sometimes without moving your hips at all.

Sometimes referred to as vaginal gymnastics, pompoir takes things a step further than your standard Kegel routine. “Think of Kegels as going to the gym and pompoir as playing the sport,” says Karen Toubi, DO, a board-certified OB/GYN and menopause and vulvovaginal disorders specialist at Lavida Women's Health, Beverly Hills.

But before you start treating your vagina like it's training for the Olympics, there's something worth knowing: Pompoir isn't about permanently tightening your vagina, performing for your partner, or guaranteeing better orgasms. It's another way to explore pleasure, provided you're doing it safely and actually enjoying yourself.

Here's how pompoir works, how to try it, and what to know before giving it a go.

Unlike regular Kegels, pompoir is less about individual squeezes and more about controlling the timing, rhythm, and intensity of those contractions during sex. The idea is to create different sensations around a partner's penis or sex toy without relying entirely on thrusting.

The muscles doing the work are the same ones you engage during Kegels, including a group called the levator ani, which helps support your pelvic organs. And while you might be able to squeeze those muscles on command, getting them to cooperate in different rhythms takes a little more finesse. “Control comes with practice, like any motor skill,” says Liz Miracle, a pelvic floor physical therapist and head of clinical quality and education at Origin.

As for what it feels like? The person practicing pompoir might notice an internal pulsing sensation, while their partner could feel anything from a gentle squeeze to a fluttering movement. Of course, everyone's experience is different, and some partners might barely notice the contractions at all.

Before trying pompoir during sex, it helps to get familiar with your pelvic floor muscles on your own. Here's where to start.

Lie on your back with your knees bent and imagine you're trying to stop yourself from peeing or passing gas. Feel that gentle squeeze and lift? That's your pelvic floor doing its thing. Just don't actually practice by stopping your pee midstream, since that can interfere with normal bladder emptying.

“Your glutes, thighs, and belly should stay quiet,” says Miracle. Focus on squeezing and fully releasing the muscles without holding your breath. If you're struggling to figure out whether you're doing it correctly, a pelvic floor physical therapist can help.

Once you've identified the right muscles, try changing up the rhythm. Dr. Toubi suggests holding a contraction for up to 10 seconds before fully relaxing, or trying quick squeezes followed by an immediate release. You can also play around with different patterns, like two slow contractions followed by a faster one.

But don't focus solely on how hard you can squeeze. Amanda Neri, PT, DPT, pelvic health expert at Smile Makers Collection, emphasizes that learning to fully relax your pelvic floor between contractions is just as important as strengthening it. Otherwise, you risk creating unnecessary tension.

According to Bryony Lees, sex and relationships educator at Little Leaf Agency, missionary, cowgirl, and spooning can all be good positions for practicing pompoir, especially if you can keep your hips relatively still. Choose whichever feels most comfortable, and have your partner stay relatively still while you experiment with different squeezes and releases.

And yes, tell your partner what you're doing. Ask what feels good, pay attention to your own sensations, and don't feel like you need to master some elaborate routine to make it worthwhile. If anything hurts, stop.

It can, depending on the person. For the person practicing it, pompoir may increase body awareness and make certain sensations more noticeable. Some people also report stronger orgasms, since the pelvic floor muscles are involved in climaxing.

“For some people, yes, it can improve sex. It isn’t a guaranteed upgrade, though, and lots of people have great sex without ever thinking about this,” says Miracle. While research suggests pelvic floor muscle training can improve sexual function, pompoir specifically hasn't been studied enough to say whether it delivers the same benefits.

And despite what you might have heard, tighter doesn't automatically mean better. In fact, an overly tense pelvic floor can make sex painful. As Dr. Toubi explains, pompoir is about developing control, not permanently tightening your vagina or proving how good you are in bed. Reminder: Sex isn't a performance review.

Generally, yes, but that doesn't mean you should spend every spare minute squeezing your pelvic floor muscles. Overdoing it can lead to muscle tension, pelvic pain, urinary urgency, and even painful sex.

“A healthy pelvic floor needs to relax as well as squeeze,” says Dr. Toubi. If your muscles are already overly tense, adding more contractions could actually make things worse.

If you experience pain during sex, have vaginismus, chronic pelvic pain, or symptoms of pelvic organ prolapse, check with a pelvic floor physical therapist before trying pompoir. The same goes for anyone experiencing urinary leakage or other pelvic floor symptoms. If you've recently given birth or had pelvic surgery, talk to your healthcare provider about when it's safe to start.

Ultimately, pompoir should be something you try because you're curious and it feels good, not because you think your vagina needs improving. There are plenty of other ways to have great sex; no vaginal workouts required.

#6769. Before & After: Dark, Underutilized Storage Spaces Make Way for a Cozy Library, Breakfast Nook, and Family Room

An intentional gut renovation turned dead space into room for a growing family.

With certain homes, it can take time (and a designer’s eye) to realize the full potential. For some, that means living within a less-than-perfect layout for a while to really understand the pain points. When a couple with twin boys bought this Greek Revival farmhouse in Connecticut’s tony Fairfield County in 2022, they knew changes were in store down the line.

“It didn’t feel like them yet,” says the project’s interior designer, Vivian Shao Chen. “The house was in great condition, but the layout didn’t work for how they lived.” The 1940s home had a considerable amount of dead space, including entire rooms and shadowy corners that felt disconnected from the rest of the home.

On top of that, planning a rework around the two youngsters was an additional challenge. “The question was always how a space can grow with them: what deserves the investment and what should stay flexible,” Chen adds.

Once Chen began working on the project in 2024, she refocused the homeowners' attention away from the idea of a home addition and sparse, “Scandinavian beach house” aesthetic toward a warmer, more traditional sensibility in line with the house’s original period style.

“I always start with trying to understand existing site conditions as much as possible,” Chen says. “What is the architectural language of the house? Where is it inconsistent, what can we edit? Often, very small but thoughtful changes can make all the difference.”

Ultimately, a gut renovation was necessary to address the home’s layout limitations, and new millwork was added throughout to provide character and a strong visual throughline. Now, each space has its own unique identity and every corner is packed with purpose—with plenty of wiggle room to allow for the young family’s necessary changes over time.

“This house feels so warm, cozy, and grounding,” Chen says. “Our hope is that we've designed for their current season of life and set a good foundation for the future without overdesigning, leaving room for the house to change as life does.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Vivian Shao ChenContractor: The O'Dell GroupStylist: Andrew Carter ThomasLocation: Weston, CTThe Space: A three-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bath home across 4,218 square feet

A formerly nondescript pass-through area, the home’s entry now gives visitors a proper greeting with its lush Sandberg wallpaper bridging indoors and out. “It speaks to the beautiful Connecticut setting we are in and also says, ‘Welcome, come in and relax, it’s not too formal here,’” notes Chen.

Additionally, choosing darker tones in a foyer or hallway is a great way to emphasize an airier mood elsewhere. “These transition spaces are meant to feel cozy, enveloping and a little moody, so when you step into the open expanse of the living room, with all its light and views, the house seems to exhale,” the designer adds.

One space that was spared a major redesign was the living room, as its existing architecture already suited the household’s needs. “The living room has always been a breath of fresh air in the house, being the first big expanse you walk into, with wonderful views out onto the property and beautiful daylight,” Chen attests.

A touch-up was still in order, though. Chen refreshed the wall color and chose updated furnishings upholstered in velvet and linen to complement the family’s antique Chinese chest, which became the natural focal point in the room. “The contrasting elements balance out the lightness of the room in a way that feels quiet and grounding,” she adds.

One of the biggest triumphs of a home renovation is when an awkward spare room becomes the new go-to gathering spot—and that’s exactly what happened with this former storage space-turned-library.

Chen removed an en suite powder room, which helped anchor the layout around a seating area instead of feeling transitory. She then wrapped the room in quarter-sawn white oak millwork, which layered in all the stately personality that the former space was missing.

The millwork, while providing shelving and storage opportunities, also included a media center, a built-in desk, and a daybed. “The daybed cushions are intentionally sized like twin mattresses, so with a fitted sheet they become an extra bed for guests or sleepovers,” Chen says.

The space is now appropriately flexible, able to be cordoned off from the main living room with pocket doors or inviting enough for games or movies, with classic library sconces that make it especially cozy at night.

“The kitchen was a gut renovation, but in reviewing the layout, I felt like the bones were mostly right,” Chen muses. “It just needed a lot of editing and new finishes.” Without altering the footprint, Chen maximized the flow of the space by removing the ceiling beams, relocating the refrigerator, scaling up the windows overlooking the yard, and adding interior windows to open up views to the adjacent mudroom and breakfast room.

Then there was the kitchen island: a solid white oak piece on custom turned legs, topped with a seamless Calacatta Monet slab that Chen calls “sublime.” Overhead, an artful single pendant by Roll & Hill adds a far more impactful ambiance than a trio ever could. “It’s about finding restraint and negative space where it can make an impact,” Chen notes.

One notable omission in the cook space is the presence of upper cabinets—Chen chose hutch cabinets instead that “hide a microwave, a pantry, and a coffee station.” Apart from the millwork, finishes, and fixtures, the kitchen also includes all-new appliances, aside from the statement-making blue La Cornue range that was already in the home.

Even if breakfasting outdoors is only realistic for a few months in the Northeast, this nook right off the kitchen now provides the illusion of alfresco enjoyment. Originally, the room felt too out of step with the rest of the home, and even its most desirable feature—the view out of the picture window—was hard to appreciate in such a segmented space.

Chen reimagined the room as an interior porch, replacing the entry wall with a muntin division to lure guests from the kitchen to this sunny spot. The nook’s newly added bay windows provide more light and dimension, and its nickel-gap paneling has a soft, rustic touch. Chen also tucked a built-in banquette into the bay for the most obvious purpose: “You're now sitting in the view, not just looking at it.”

Through the breakfast room (and another set of muntin dividers) is the cozy, cocooning dining room. Rather than flooding this windowless room with light, Chen employed a clever strategy to maximize the ambiance. “The custom plaster finish on the walls and ceiling has a subtle sheen that catches the light, so what was once the room's drawback is now its greatest charm,” she notes.

Basements without a proper design outlook are destined to become clutter overflow areas. That’s exactly what was happening on the home’s lower level, so a gut renovation was in order.

To make the space more livable, Chen added millwork to conceal laundry and utility access, plush furnishings, and a new door and windows that open up to a walk-out terrace. Though the low ceiling had to stay, new nickel-gap paneling makes the space feel cozy rather than cramped. “The room reads as a true family room,” she says.

The former mudroom was a rough-and-tumble catch-all passage, but considering that this area functions as the family’s main entrance, it deserved a gut renovation that they could take pride in—starting with the floor. Chen swapped out the no-nonsense tile for a striking limestone checkerboard. “The light squares are actually the same limestone as the kitchen floor, only done in a different pattern,” she says.

New lockers and coat cabinets along the perimeter conceal all the out-the-door essentials, while a floating console, clad in drab olive, has the look of an antique find but was custom-made for the space. It functions as the necessary landing zone that every bustling family home needs; Chen intentionally kept the bottom open for sliding packages and storage baskets (and perhaps the odd shoe) out of the way.

This office bathroom was previously awash in neutrals but still managed to look dark and drab, so Chen revitalized it with a much-needed dose of drama. Her go-to design reference? Old New York members’ clubs.

“Instead of wrapping the room in dark wood, we kept the stained wood to the custom vanity and let color and pattern do the rest,” she notes. Outfitted with the white penny-tile floor, striped zellige in the shower, and racing-green color drenching, the space now nods to tradition while keeping it playful.

“The primary bedroom already had so much going for it: beautiful windows, generous proportions, and absolutely peaceful,” Chen attests. “Our goal was to keep and elevate those qualities.”

Chen amplified the room’s natural daylight with a fresh coat of Farrow & Ball's Slipper Satin, then softened the space with more natural textures, including a slipcovered linen bed, woven linen for the drapery, and a textured wool rug. Even though the bedroom has one of the most restrained palettes in the whole home, it celebrates textural variation.

To maintain the same serene but texturally rich feel as the adjoining primary bedroom, the bathroom needed a layout and material rework. To make the most of a modest footprint, Chen opted for slim Shaker linen cabinets and a floating vanity. “Seeing the floor continue beneath it tricks the eye, making the room feel larger than its actual standing space,” she says.

The room’s warm marble floors and soft off-white color also provide an ideal backdrop for the room’s showstopper moment: a Calacatta Viola slab shower and backsplash, with deep, wine-toned veining.

For now, the twin boys share this bedroom, so the existing architecture was left alone. Instead, the focus was on enriching the palette with taupe, cream, blue, and copper tones and choosing furnishings that can flex over time. “The art is by the boys themselves, which was so special to incorporate into the room,” says Chen.

Vivian Shao Chen is an interior designer specializing in whole-home designs, including interior architecture. She has over 15 years of architectural training and professional experience, and focuses on residential interiors and lifestyle design, including home goods and clothing.

#6766. Your Sexual Boundaries Aren't Up for Debate, Even in BDSM

Hard limits, soft limits, and why knowing the difference matters.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

There's a pretty big difference between being open to trying something new in bed and being actually okay with it. Maybe you're curious about being tied up but aren't ready to hand over control just yet. Or perhaps dirty talk is your thing, but certain words are an immediate turnoff. Whatever your preferences, knowing where you draw the line is important, especially when BDSM and kink enter the picture.

Despite what some sexy movies might make it seem, BDSM—which stands for bondage and discipline, Dominance and submission, and/or sadism and masochism—isn’t just a free-for-all to get freaky. Instead, hard and soft limits help establish what's completely off the table, what you might be open to exploring, and what needs to happen before anyone considers trying something new.

“There's this idea that talking about sex beforehand somehow takes away from the spontaneity or excitement. I actually think the opposite. Having these conversations is what allows people to feel safe enough to explore,” says Sarah Kelleher, LCSW, a sex and relationship psychotherapist.

After all, the appeal of kink might involve stepping outside your comfort zone, but that doesn't mean anyone gets to decide where your boundaries are except you.

Ahead, sex experts explain the difference between hard and soft limits, how to figure out yours, and how to talk about them with a partner before things get interesting.

In BDSM, limits are the boundaries that determine what you're comfortable doing during sexual play, what you're not interested in, and what might require a little more discussion. They're especially important when exploring activities involving power, control, or physical sensations, where one person's idea of a good time might be another person's absolute nightmare.

Hard limits are firm, non-negotiable boundaries. They're the things you absolutely don't want to do, regardless of the circumstances. For example, someone might be comfortable with light spanking but draw the line at anything that leaves bruises. Others might rule out humiliation, certain types of penetration, or specific language during dirty talk. Whatever the reason, a hard limit means no. Full stop.

Soft limits, on the other hand, are conditional boundaries. These are activities you might be curious about but aren't necessarily ready or willing to try without certain conditions in place. Maybe hair pulling is off-limits unless it's a gentle tug, or you're curious about bondage but only willing to explore it with loose, soft ties.

Importantly, a soft limit isn't permission to keep asking until someone changes their mind. “'Soft' doesn't mean optional, negotiable by default, or something that can be ignored,” says Lisa Finn, a sex educator at Babeland. Until someone explicitly agrees to explore an activity, that boundary still stands.

And while limits are particularly important in BDSM, they're not exclusive to kink. Everyone has sexual boundaries, whether they're experimenting with elaborate power dynamics or sticking to their favorite tried-and-true positions.

Figuring out your boundaries isn't always as simple as knowing what turns you on and what doesn't. Especially if you're new to kink, you might be curious about something in theory but have no idea how you'd feel about it in practice. Don’t stress. You don't need to have an entire BDSM bucket list figured out before you start exploring.

One place to start is with a Yes/No/Maybe list, which breaks down different sexual activities into things you're interested in, things you're definitely not interested in, and things you might consider under the right circumstances. “Your ‘maybes’ are often where your soft limits live,” says Sarah Tomchesson, a certified sex educator and director of marketing and education at Magic Wand.

But don't assume every maybe is secretly a yes waiting to happen. Kelleher recommends getting curious about why something makes you hesitant. Are you nervous because it's unfamiliar, or because you genuinely don't want to do it? There's a difference between feeling excited about a new experience and feeling like you should be excited about it.

It can also help to pay attention to how your body responds when you imagine certain activities. Tomchesson suggests noticing changes in your breathing, tension, or overall sense of comfort. You can also leave something in the maybe category indefinitely. There's no deadline for figuring out your kinks, and you're not obligated to try something just because it sounds interesting.

The best time to talk about your sexual boundaries? Before you're naked, turned on, or halfway through a scene. While discussing your limits might not sound like the sexiest form of foreplay, it's a lot easier to make informed decisions when nobody's caught up in the moment.

Start by talking about what you're interested in exploring, what's completely off-limits, and what you'd only consider under certain circumstances. Be specific, too. If you're comfortable with dirty talk but certain words are off-limits, say exactly which ones. And make sure you're asking about your partner's boundaries just as much as you're sharing your own.

Before getting started, you'll also want to establish a safeword or another clear signal that means it's time to stop. Finn recommends the traffic light system: Green means you're comfortable continuing, yellow means you need to slow down or pause and check in, and red means stop immediately. Nonverbal signals are also important for situations where speaking might be difficult or impossible.

And what if your partner's limits don't line up with yours? That doesn't mean someone needs to give in. “I actually prefer the word collaboration over compromise when we're talking about sex,” says Kelleher. Rather than trying to convince someone to participate in an activity they're uncomfortable with, look for other ways to explore the desires you share.

Finally, keep the conversation going after sex. Tomchesson recommends checking in about what felt good, what didn't, and what you'd like to do differently next time. Your boundaries aren't a one-time discussion, and neither is consent.

Absolutely. Your boundaries can change over time, but that doesn't mean they have to. Maybe a kink you once found intimidating starts to sound appealing, or something you used to love no longer does it for you. Both are completely normal, and neither requires an explanation.

“Our sexuality isn't static, and neither are our boundaries,” says Kelleher. As your experiences, relationships, and interests evolve, your limits might shift, too. Some maybes stay maybes forever, and that's perfectly fine.

If you're interested in revisiting a soft limit, have that conversation with your partner outside of sexual play. Discuss what you're curious about, what conditions would help you feel comfortable, and what you'd like to keep off-limits. Agreeing to try something once doesn't mean you've signed up for it indefinitely. Consent can be withdrawn at any time.

There's also a big difference between someone willingly exploring a boundary and a partner pressuring them to change it. “A soft limit isn't a boundary that's meant to be pushed,” says Kelleher. Repeatedly asking, bargaining, or making someone feel guilty for saying no isn't healthy sexual exploration.

If a partner crosses an established limit, stop the activity immediately. Sometimes boundaries are crossed through genuine misunderstandings, but intentionally ignoring a limit or safeword is a consent violation. You get to decide what happens next, whether that's having a conversation, taking space, or ending the relationship.

Your limits belong to you, and you don't owe anyone a more adventurous version of yourself. The whole point of exploring your sexuality is figuring out what feels good to you, not convincing yourself to enjoy something for someone else's benefit.

#6765. You Don’t Need Designer Sneakers. You Need These $95 German Army Trainers.

We must admit that these ones from Quince are damn good.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

In my life, I lean toward luxury and secondhand clothing wherever possible, desperately trying to get away from all the cheap garbage being sold to me on Instagram ads and the Amazon homepage. I’ve spent years of my life hunting sales and eBay listings for clothes and boots. I’m like an edge-habitat pine growing in the direction of the radiant sun that is well-made clothing, trying to forget the fast fashion slop that lies in the forest behind me.

But I throw that out the window when it comes to sneakers. Designer sneakers are, for me, one of the dumbest things I could ever buy. Even when they are made with real leather, you are paying for a plastic sole that has a lifespan of a few years. I don’t think you should pay more than the $200 price of a pair of New Balance when it comes to sneakers.

Under that price point, I’ve never been able to find a good pair German Army Trainers, which I have always wanted. That changed when I found these. Quince (yeah yeah, I was just complaining about fashion slop, but life is full of contradictions) makes a perfect pair of GATs—and it makes them for $95. I honestly think every man in the world should buy a pair of these.

This is my real qualm with designer sneakers, and it totally undercuts anything you could tell me about the second-hand sneaker hype market. What you should pay for, when you pay expensive prices for clothing, is craftsmanship. When you buy a Gucci 1953 loafer or Loro Piana cashmere you probably arrived there because of the brand, but you are paying for the craftsmanship. I see no such correlation in designer sneakers, even the ones those brands make.

But a $95 pair of kicks? That’s honest. Even though I’m not bullish on Quince’s fast fashion take on quiet luxury (nor am I bullish on its caviar program), and the fact that Quince chose to closely replicate the Margiela GAT instead of the original German army models, I will let it slide. At $95, there are no claims being made about leather quality, or the footbed, or whatever is going on in the sole you can’t see. They look good and are easy to replace; it’s about as honest of a deal as you can find shopping these days.

The thing about the original BW Sport GATs—which you can get for about this price still, though used workout sneakers aren’t my cup of tea—is that they’re just about bulletproof. Those original mid-century designs made were first issued in the 1970s and stopped being issued around the end of the Cold War, a real feat of durability.

These shoes mimic that Army-issued durability. I’ve worn these sneakers for going on a year now. In that time, I’ve seen many pairs of more expensive kicks start to fall apart, get scuffed beyond recognition, and stink like hell. These have not. The pair I’ve worn all year is the pair pictured in this story. Nothing I say in this endorsement could be more convincing than that.

And really that’s the argument here. When I wrote about the Chuck Taylors I normally wear, it was a use-it-and-lose-it argument—they cost $45, who cares if the soles get holes within 12 months. But here, I fear I’m going to sound like a marketing mouthpiece for Quince. These shoes are $95, but they’ll last just as long as a pair that cost you $950. That’s all you can ask for these days.

#6764. 12 Things You Should Toss From Your Kitchen ASAP Before Thanksgiving

Experts say a quick kitchen edit now can save holiday stress later.

It’ll soon be the most important day of the year in the kitchen, so give yourself the gift of space and freedom from clutter by doing a quick purge.

“Thanksgiving needs every inch of counter, fridge, and cabinet space you’ve got,” says Ann Lightfoot, cofounder of DDH Home Organizing & Move Management. “A short edit beforehand means you can find the baster when you need it, the leftovers have somewhere to go, and you aren’t digging past three broken whisks to reach a serving spoon.”

But don’t overdo it! “You don’t need a Pinterest-perfect pantry. You need room to work,” says Lightfoot. “A full organizing project can easily turn into a weekend of half-finished bins, and that’s the last thing anyone needs in November.”

Instead of tearing apart the entire kitchen, focus on the areas and items that will have the most impact. “We always say edit before you organize, and this is the editing part: take things out,” says Lightfoot. “Do one quick pass, ask whether each item earns its space, and let the rest go. Labels and matching containers can wait until January.”

Ahead, here’s what experts say to get rid of ASAP so your kitchen will be ready for cooking and entertaining at Thanksgiving.

“Spices lose their flavor over time, and stale cinnamon or a two-year-old jar of sage will make itself known in your pie and stuffing,” says Lightfoot. “Check the dates on baking soda, baking powder, and flour too. Toss what’s past its prime and make a shopping list while you’re at it.”

Glasses with scratches just don’t look great. “I'm all for mixing vintage and contemporary pieces, but damaged drinking glasses and dishes should go,” says Kerrie Kelly, NKBA, FASID, CEO and creative director of Kerrie Kelly Studio, and NKBA 2026 National Board Chair. “Holiday entertaining is a wonderful excuse to take inventory and make sure you have enough functional pieces for your guests.”

“Three napkins and a stack of faded plates won't set a beautiful table. Let them go and start fresh,” says Nikki Boyd, organizing expert, author of Beautifully Organized, and founder of At Home With Nikki.

It’s amazing how stuff gets pushed to the back of the fridge, never to be seen again. “Fridge space is prime real estate on Thanksgiving, between the turkey, the sides, and everything guests bring,” says Lightfoot. “Clear out the half-empty dressings, the jam from last summer, and anything you can’t identify.”

Be honest with yourself. “If it hasn't come out for a single holiday in years, it's not waiting for this one,” says Boyd. “Keep heirlooms with stories, and release the rest.”

“Kitchen drawers have a way of collecting multiples of things we rarely need more than one of,” says Laura Kinsella, founder of Urban OrgaNYze. “Pull out your spatulas, peelers, measuring cups, and all those cooking tools that always seem to get stuck in the drawer. If you're not reaching for them when you're hosting 20 people, chances are you don't need them for a regular weeknight dinner either.”

“Your table is your stage. Burnt down candles and stained runners don't belong on it,” says Boyd.

Containers that have no lids need to go away ASAP. Ditto for lids that don’t have a container. “Before the holiday leftovers start piling up, take 10 minutes to match your food storage containers with their lids. Get rid of anything that's cracked, warped, or missing its match,” says Kinsella. “There's nothing more frustrating than having a refrigerator full of leftovers and realizing you can't find a single lid that fits!”

That bread maker you haven’t touched lately? The air fryer that’s collecting dust? It’s time to donate them. “Any big kitchenware item that you haven’t used for the past two to three holiday seasons needs to go,” says Amelia Meena, owner of Appleshine, Organization + Design.

If they’re in bad shape, treat yourself to some new ones. “Nonstick pans with a flaking coating and sheet pans warped beyond saving make cooking harder than it has to be,” says Lightfoot. “If you’ve been meaning to replace one, do it now so you aren’t stuck mid-recipe.”

Soy sauce and ketchup packets, plastic forks, extra napkins, and the stack of menus add up quickly. You’ll want that drawer space for foil, twine, and the meat thermometer, so toss the whole lot into the garbage immediately, says Lightfoot.

Stained, threadbare towels and thin pot holders aren’t safe for pulling a hot roasting pan out of the oven—plus, raggedy mitts don’t exactly scream “festive.” Pitch thin pot holders and recycle old towels into cleaning rags, then treat yourself to a cute new set of kitchen linens, says Lightfoot.

#6763. Fascia Release Is the Key to Regulating Your Nervous System, Getting Better Sleep, and Improving Skin

Fascia, the thin web that envelops the face and body, is more powerful than you might think. Here’s how to release it

I’m curled up on a treatment bed in the Corinthia spa, feeling a knot of tension dissipate slowly from my neck and shoulders. Within minutes, tears prick my eyes. I’m familiar with the sudden catharsis bodywork can bring, but this is different. Known as “myofascial release”—a targeted therapy that works with fascia, the continuous matrix of connective tissue that wraps every muscle, nerve, and organ—it aims to reduce tightness and release restricted tissue, restoring its natural glide.

“Fascia is deeply connected to the entire sensory network,” explains Nadia Alibhai, a London-based osteopath who was unfazed by my heightened emotions. “When you move or hold stress, forces are transmitted through this interconnected system. It senses everything and dictates how the whole body responds. This treatment directly influences how we perceive and react to tension.”

Once dismissed as mere biological wrapping paper, fascia—made up of water, collagen, elastin, and hyaluronic acid—is now understood as a living, three-dimensional web. “It’s part of a complex system involving movement, circulation, lymphatics, posture, and tissue health,” Alibhai explains. When it works well, it keeps everything moving and prevents systems from becoming sluggish, but when it becomes restricted, often from scrolling, sedentary habits, or chronic stress, the fluid between the layers can thicken. This affects physical mobility and emotional well-being, leading to issues ranging from pain and stiffness to a dysregulated nervous system.

Fascia and neurology are intrinsically linked, explains skin-health expert Annee de Mamiel. “Fascia changes according to how we move, breathe, and repeatedly load the body,” she says. “Cortisol and adrenaline change our physiology; the nervous system alters breathing, muscular tone, and stress response. Over time, these repeated patterns affect the tissues.” It’s a catch-22: greater stress can tighten fascia, which in turn can send your body into fight-or-flight mode, prolonging the anxiety that caused the damage in the first place.

This mind-body connection is just one reason why fascia has piqued the wellness industry’s interest. Google searches for “fascia release” have soared in the past year, while facials targeting the system are becoming more popular. Robert Schleip, the director of the Fascia Research Group at Ulm University, attributes this to its holistic possibilities. “Fascia is now studied in relation to the autonomic nervous system, and therefore emotional regulation, as well as the immune system and aging,” he says. The latest findings show that supporting your fascia goes far beyond easing a tight neck; it's linked to reduced arterial stiffness and improved cardiovascular health.

Fascia release has also entered the skin-health conversation. Tight fascia can be an overlooked culprit behind loss of luminosity, puffiness, and premature aging, says de Mamiel, because it’s heavily involved in fluid drainage and lymphatic pathways. “The diaphragm plays a role in pressure changes and fluid movement through the body, and the fascial system connects these areas,” she explains. “When I work on movement and tension through the neck, chest, and rib cage, I often see a change in how congested or puffy the face appears.” However, releasing fascia effectively doesn't require aggressive force. “If I use so much pressure that your body braces, I’ve produced the opposite response—it’s about creating conditions where the nervous system and tissue can both settle.”

Facialist Nichola Joss achieves similarly impressive results with her signature Inner Facial, which fuses lymphatic drainage, fascia release, buccal massage, and sculpting techniques. “Fascia surrounds and supports our muscles; when restricted, it affects how freely muscles move,” Joss explains. This affects lymphatic flow and your skin’s natural glow. “By releasing muscular and fascial tension, you lift, tone, and sculpt contours while boosting circulation and softening fine lines.”

Beyond professional treatments, supporting fascial health depends on consistent daily habits. First, fascia thrives on varied movement: try walking, twisting your body, reaching overhead, and squatting to support overall health. Unfortunately, there are no true quick fixes: Spending the day glued to an office chair and then going straight into an intense HIIT session can actually be counterproductive. Instead, interrupt patterns throughout the day: Stand during phone calls instead of sitting, swing your arms gently during screen breaks, and make sure you’re not holding your breath during stressful periods at work. Counteract hours spent hunched over a device with this simple doorway stretch: Place your forearms against the frame, step forward gently, and hold for 20 to 30 seconds while breathing deeply.

Schleip recommends aligning exercise with your circadian rhythm. “Incorporate dynamic, elastic, or bouncing movements in the morning, ideally outdoors, and slow stretches in the evening,” he suggests, adding that sustained yoga positions, such as a child’s pose held for at least 30 seconds, can be particularly helpful. “Regular stretching improves joint mobility and may benefit cardiovascular markers such as arterial stiffness,” he adds. Finally, make nutrition and hydration a priority: fascia is 70 percent water and needs hydration to glide freely.

As Alibhai reflects at the end of my session: “The goal isn’t to constantly release fascia; it’s to keep your body moving, adaptable, and well nourished.” I leave feeling lighter and more grounded—and I instinctively skip the Tube and walk home.

#6754. 6 Stylish Ways Designers Make Vintage Tile the Star of the Room

Here’s how to give this charming, old-school detail new life.

When we redesign, remodel, and renovate, it can be tempting to update, replace, and modernize every nook and cranny of your home. But the goal is rarely to chase newness. It’s easy to see vintage details, like quirky tile, as an obstacle standing between your home and a modern look, but tearing it out might make your heart ache later.

“I love the irregularities you find in older tiles, whether it's subtle variations in color, an imperfect glaze, or the undulation of a tile's surface. Those imperfections are what make them so beautiful, giving a room texture, character, and a sense of craftsmanship,” says Ali Burgoon Nolan, founder and principal designer at Studio Burgoon. Tile is one of the vintage details homeowners regret removing the most, as modern-day designs and materials lack the panache, durability, and craftsmanship of decades past. Not to mention, the removal and reinstallation will cost you a big chunk of your budget.

“Just like in other areas of the home, introducing a vintage element helps to create more interesting, storied spaces,” says Johanna Lyle, founder of Jo Lyle & Co. “When we mix elements from different eras, the home feels more unique and collected over time. If you've bought an old home and aren't sure you love the existing, perhaps historic, tile, try to change some things around it before you give up on it completely. You might not dig it just because you haven't seen it before, or it's not what is currently popular.”

If you’re lucky enough to have inherited vintage tile in your historic home, here are six ways designers have given it the main character energy it deserves.

Jennie Bishop preserved the charm of this century-old Illinois foyer by buffing the shine back into the home’s original tile. Her team then refinished the adjacent wood staircase and painted the walls a creamy white to brighten the room.

Designer Galena Younger knows potential when she sees it, and this historic cottage in San Antonio was no exception. The home’s original Mexican-style tiles surround the living room hearth, complemented by family heirlooms scattered around the room for an ultrapersonal, deliciously vintage aesthetic.

Interior and jewelry designer Trish Becker preserved this home’s original tile mantel, using it as the starting point for the room’s design. A corn-husk woven rug adds earthy warmth, while touches of green connect the room’s architectural details to its bold patterned slipper chairs.

With the goal of restoring her home’s “1931 glory,” designer Claire Thomas was ecstatic to find this bathroom’s original purple untouched, despite many renovations by previous owners. Playing on the hue, she brought in soft floral wallpaper and skirted purple stools for a feminine edge, finishing the room with a sage vanity for a pop of color and soft contrast.

Artist Dana Gibson and interior designer Sara Hillery brought a playful touch to this sunroom, keeping the home’s original tile floors as the foundation. They drew out the tiles’ blue tones in the plush textiles, then added woven furniture to complement its earthy browns.

It doesn’t get more classic than a black-and-white design scheme. In this Richmond bath, Gibson and Hillery leaned into the existing retro tiles, opting to carry the palette through the room’s entire design.

#6752. Where Are Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison Now? Here’s What Happened Since the Events of ‘The Altruists’

Is there potential for a You Can See Everything-ish update to The Altruists? Where are Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison now? The Altruists ends with Bankman-Fried (Anthony Boyle) getting arrested just before getting to tell Ellison (Julia Garner) that he loves her over the phone. What happened next, in real life, is hopefully not all that sequel-worthy.

Following the cut to black, there are some updates, as based-on-a-true-story shows and movies often include just before the credits. If you turned Netflix off and didn’t read them, here’s what they said:

But still, that was years ago! Did The Altruists leave anything out? Here’s an update as to what else Bankman-Fried and Ellison have been doing.

Bankman-Fried began serving his penalty on December 12, 2022. He is imprisoned at Federal Correctional Institution, Lompoc I in California. Fun (?) fact: Changpeng Zhao aka CZ, Bankman-Fried’s former investor who also appears on The Altruists, served four months at the same prison in 2024.

Ellison was sentenced to 24 months in federal prison and released after 14 months at the beginning of 2026. She was held at Federal Correctional Institution, Danbury in Connecticut. That particular prison has a whole history of fun facts. It’s where Piper Kerman, the author of Orange Is the New Black, served time. That’s right, this prison is the “real” Litchfield. It has also appeared in popular culture multiple times and other infamous inmates IRL include Steve Bannon, Lauryn Hill, and Teresa Giudice.

Over the summer, Ellison was discreetly hired at Manifund, a nonprofit for matching donors with charity organizations. The company’s CEO, Austin Chen, pledged to give her a second chance, and praised Ellison’s “relatable” personal writing on his own Substack. He noted that FTX gave him seed money, and that he was one of the victims of Bankman-Fried and Ellison’s crimes. (And just flagging for work-life balance reasons that he also said she gets along great with his young daughter.)

“I understand that some of our users, donors, and partners may now be skittish about working with Manifund, given this announcement,” Chen concludes. “I appreciate any trust you are willing to extend, and ask that you judge us by our historical and future outputs. You (or your LLM) can take a look at Manifund’s source code, underlying data, finances and research; if you wish, you can see Caroline’s specific code commits and comments on Manifund.”

So that’s what she’s up to; potentially turning over a new leaf at a new company. Or potentially repeating the same problematic patterns with new colleagues. Surely someone so math-savvy would notice that before it happened. Right?

#6750. Omega and Swatch Just Launched a New MoonSwatch—and No Two Are the Same

The Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake, the 39th watch in the series, will have collectors keeping an eye on the Swiss weather forecast.

By MoonSwatch standards, it’s been a quiet year. Swatch has been otherwise occupied with the Audemars Piguet Royal Pop, the May release for which people lined up for six days in New York, police deployed tear gas in France, and which, according to Swatch Group, has generated more than 25 billion views on social media.

Not that interest in the MoonSwatch has gone away.

An estimated 4.5 million have been sold since the collaboration began in March 2022 (and that figure only takes us to the end of 2024). In July this year, some 1.2 million people applied to buy one of the 1,969 examples of its $570 Mission to the Moon 1969, a watch that incorporated 11 grams of 18-carat gold recycled from vintage Omega components.

The latest MoonSwatch—only the third new model this year, compared with 11 in 2023—is the Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake.

An all-black counterpart to last winter’s all-white Cold Moon edition, it takes as its starting point the idea that no two snowflakes are alike.

Swatch has developed a digital process capable of generating millions of different snowflake patterns, one of which is laser-engraved onto the moonphase indicator of each watch. So, unusually for a mass-produced quartz watch, every example is unique.

It also features the complication Swatch introduced in November 2024 and which remains exclusive to the MoonSwatch: an earthphase indicator. Where a conventional moonphase shows the phases of the Moon as viewed from Earth, this does the reverse. The two displays run in opposite directions over the same 29.5-day cycle.

(How useful this is to those of us who live on Earth is debatable, but it’s one of the more original ideas in watchmaking in recent years.)

There are some nice details elsewhere on the 42mm Bioceramic watch, including Snoopy and Woodstock in the snow, a pair of miniature skis at 11 o’clock, and a tiny patch of white on the Earth representing a snow-covered Switzerland, which glows green under UV light.

As with last year’s winter edition, there’s a stipulation.

The watch goes on sale at selected Swatch stores worldwide on Black Friday, November 27. After that, until March 20, 2027, the last day of winter, you’ll only be able to buy one when it’s snowing in Switzerland.

Swatch will be providing weather updates on its website.

#6748. Curtain Bangs Are an Elegant, Low-Maintenance Style for Mature Hair: How to Wear It Now

Timeless and face-framing, this is the easiest way to update your haircut

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From Sabrina Carpenter to Dakota Johnson, curtain bangs were among the most fawned-over hairstyles in the past few years. It’s not surprising; they flatter all face shapes and offer the face-framing effect of a fringe without the harshness (or any awkward growing-out stage) of a full, blunt chop.

Curtain bangs are also versatile at any age, and the cut has recently stepped into the spotlight as one of the most flattering styles for mature lengths. Penélope Cruz’s recent red-carpet appearance makes a convincing case for the look.

"Curtain bangs are one of the most flattering fringe options for mature hair because they soften the face rather than dominate it," explains hairdresser David Cassidy from Hove's Palette & Bristle. "They frame the cheekbones, draw attention to the eyes, and create movement around the front of the hair, which instantly feels more youthful."

Unlike a blunt fringe, he adds that curtain bangs blend into the rest of the haircut, creating an elegant, modern look with just the right amount of youthfulness.

Here, he explains how to get the perfect curtain fringe for mature hair this winter, along with key styling pointers.

As with any bangs cut, an expert eye and professional hand are key to achieving a look that suits your face shape.

"The key with curtain bangs on mature hair is softness and proportion," says Cassidy. "You want them to start no shorter than the cheekbone and taper gently into the length. Anything too short or too heavy can feel aging."

He also emphasizes texture; a slightly broken-up finish keeps them from looking flat and gives a more relaxed, lived-in effect.

"Before committing to curtain bangs, think about your natural hair texture and growth pattern," he adds. "Cowlicks, partings, and how your hair falls when it air-dries all matter. A good stylist will tailor the fringe to work with your hair, not against it. Curtain bangs are versatile. When done well, they grow out seamlessly and can be worn parted, swept back, or tucked away."

Before you veer away from your signature everyday style, consider how much extra styling time a new fringe might require.

"Curtain bangs are surprisingly low-maintenance if they’re cut properly," says Cassidy. "A medium round brush or even a large Velcro roller at the front while the hair cools will give that soft sweep away from the face."

He recommends styling your curtain bangs with the hair dryer angled side to side rather than straight down. "It creates lift without stiffness and keeps your bangs looking light and flattering."

#6746. The 6 Best Gaming Headsets for All Types of Players

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re playing single-player or online, these will level up your game.

Nothing truly makes me feel immersed in a game the same way good sound does. And it’s impossible to get that level of tension in a horror game like Resident Evil, or the feeling of being absolutely locked in a competitive shooter without a good pair of headphones. Nowadays, video games are fully open world of 3D and spatial audio, and there's never been a better time to hop on the bandwagon. It's time to get a gaming headset.

If you consider gaming your main hobby, you’re probably either a single-player person or you like to squad up in your battle royale of choice. It doesn’t matter, actually, because the only proper way to experience both ends of the gaming spectrum is with a headset. For single-player, that means fully immersive audio. For multiplayer, you’ll sound crystal clear to your teammates—no more confusion or excuses. The good noise-canceling headsets even have transparency modes so you don’t have to ignore everyone in your real life either.

Here's the problem. There are actually plenty of good headsets and trustworthy brands that make dozens of similar options. Finding the headset that will work for you isn’t made to be easy, but that’s why I’m here. I know my gaming headsets like the back of my hand—that’s my job. To figure out if you need a high-end wireless SteelSeries headset, or if you’ll be happy with a sub-$100 option, read on. Hell, maybe you’d prefer earbuds. I’ve accounted for all options.

This is the one gaming headset I won’t (and frankly can’t) say anything negative about. I suppose that’s what $600 gets you. SteelSeries has long made comfortable headsets that support multiple platforms with easy swapping. It introduced the world to its GameHub base station, letting you connect all your systems to one panel and easily control them all from there.

The Nova Elite takes every already perfectly solved puzzle piece and puts a little extra sheen on it. First off, it is gorgeous, with exteriors made from premium steel, aluminum, and vegan leather. Nothing else in the gaming space compares to it in terms of the luxe look. In terms of fit, the Nova Elite distributes its weight evenly so you can play for hours without discomfort.

And this headset’s gaming hub has an extra feature that really changes things. The hot-swappable batteries are SteelSeries’ biggest innovation in recent years, and here the hub also acts as a charger for your second (included) headset battery. So whichever one is not powering your current session is being charged. What this means is battery life is a solved problem. You literally never have to worry about running out of charge mid-game again. And for some, that alone might be worth the price of admission.

Okay, forget what I just said. This is everything a wireless headset needs to be for the average gamer. ASUS has targeted a lower price point and succeeded in terms of knowing which features to cut, and what to keep to make it still feel like a high-quality experience. You don't get active noise cancellation or the ability to play audio from two sources at once. For those luxuries, go for the Delta II.

At 0.7 pounds, the Pelta is remarkably light and the furthest thing from bulky. It's about letting you play all day on those summer Saturdays where it's too hot to go outside. Where it surprises in this regard is battery life. Offering up to 70 hours between charges and a fit that won't leave you itchy and sweaty, this is the all-nighter-friendly headset.

While I wouldn't personally spring for a wired headset, those who do are hard to convince otherwise. And that crowd has spent the last couple of years rallying around the HyperX Cloud Alpha. If competitive gaming is the reason you are turning to a headset, this has everything you need for under $100.

You aren't going to get the audio options you do from the multi-hundred-dollar headsets SteelSeries and Corsair sell. Instead, you are paying a reasonable price for a high-quality microphone, and a comfortable pair of headphones. Stop worrying about lag and stop worrying about your headset running out of battery. It's time to wire in.

When it comes to sound quality, the Corsair Virtuoso Max brings it to a new level. This is a better-sounding headset than most of the readers of this article have ever experienced.

My favorite high-tech touch that we actually use is the simultaneous connection. Using the 2.4 GHz dongle and Bluetooth in tandem you can get the audio from your game and audio from a Bluetooth source at the same time. That means pumping in your own tunes or a background podcast while grinding levels. When playing with friends, I love the addition of a physical, sturdy, functional mic mute button.

The only issue is long-term comfort. Weighing in at just under a pound, the 60-hour battery life on the headset is a bit of a moot point. You'll want to take it off anyway because it can start to feel quite hard on your skull after a couple hours of white knuckle gaming.

If you have a single gaming machine and that console is a PS5, this is the headset I recommend. It is remarkably lightweight, weighing even less than the wired-only pair I recommended earlier, and it’s super easy to use—with a satisfying mute button that pops in and out, and a microphone that is entirely detachable. So if you’re settling in for a night of solo horror gaming, you can set the mic aside and still enjoy the excellent sound and spatial audio options. And if you know that battery is low, or you really just want the easiest solution, the package includes a 3.5mm cable for wired connection. Just plug that right into your DualSense controller and you’re off to the races.

Beyond just PlayStation diehards, this is an excellent option for any PC or Nintendo gamer who wants a light wireless headset. It is fully compatible via USB-C transceiver or headphone cable with any other modern console, sans the Xbox.

Okay, so I lied. When you clicked on this for the six best headsets, yeah one of these isn't a gaming headset at all. It's a set of earbuds designed by SteelSeries to be used on gaming consoles and it's as much of a game-changer as it sounds. If you feel cheated, don't fret. There are two more headsets listed in the honorable mentions below. Please accept my humble offering.

If you’ll allow me to talk about them for just a minute though, the Arctis GameBuds are just shy of a miracle. They are comfy earbuds with all-encompassing noise-canceling (gasp!), spatial audio support, and a very useful transparency/talk-through mode. The sound isn't quite on par with SteelSeries's larger headsets, but it still impresses. Plus, unlike the rest of these headsets, you can fit them in your pocket.

SteelSeries Arctis Nova 7 ($179): You don't need to shell out for the Nova Elite to get a good Arctis headset. This one uses a simple USB-C dongle, so isn't nearly as complicated to set up, either.

Turtle Beach Stealth Pro 2: ($349): My favorite new headset of the year. An excellent multi-platform choice with high-quality playback and call audio. It also uses a swappable battery system, so you never run out of charge in a heated gaming moment.

Testing gaming headsets really isn't too difficult. You make sure it's compatible with what it says on the box, and then tell the people if it’s comfortable and if it sounds good. Shit simple until you do it enough to realize a simple truth. Most models from reputable brands are worth their costs, and very few companies have duds in their lineups. That said, there are ways to tell what's going to rise from the crop. Here's some pointers for the next time you shop for headsets.

Comfort — If you can't stay comfortable in them for more than an hour they are going straight in the trash. Even the most uncomfortable among these picks are still in the top 5% of comfortable gaming headphones.

Sound Quality — Like with the pair of headphones you use to listen to music, you want clear sound with plenty of range.

Microphone — If you're gaming online, your microphone needs to cut out unwanted background noise. Some offer noise cancelling, some just have good unidirectional builds. All on this list are great in this department.

Directional Sound — Spatial audio is cool when watching movies, but when playing games this can take your team play to the next level. When you hear an enemy's footsteps, you can know they're behind you at four o'clock, not six. And that makes you an asset. It also makes the taste of victory that much sweeter.

Connection — I will discuss wireless vs. wired shortly, but when it comes to the going wireless there's usually two ways to connect a gaming headset. Bluetooth or via a USB dongle. The latter typically has less latency and is the preferred way to connect.

Are wired or wireless headsets better for gaming?

If you care about latency (aka lag), we are going to recommend wired over wireless. The added hassle of wireless charging and connecting doesn't necessarily make up for the hassle of having a wire. Plus, you don't have to worry about it not working on some devices—which you do with pretty much every wireless device you buy.

Do all gaming headsets work across all platforms?

I'm glad you asked. No! As you may have noticed, there tend to be two kinds of headsets. Those that work with Xbox.. and those that work with everything else. Since Xbox uses its own wireless standard instead of Bluetooth. In practice, this means Xbox gamers need to be extra careful when buying headsets to make sure the one they're getting is compatible. SteelSeries, for example, sells two versions of each headset, one marked for Xbox.

It's actually more complicated than "all headsets that work with PC also work with PlayStation and you have to get ones especially for Xbox," but in many of these cases that is the gist. Although the PlayStation headsets work on PC and Mac but not Nintendo Switch. So, yeah, it's pretty inconsistent. Seriously, just read the list of compatible platforms before you buy a headset.

#6743. Meet the Cast and Characters of ‘Blade Runner 2099’

The ’80s film Blade Runner is the latest to get the streaming series treatment, after the legacy sequel Blade Runner 2049 revitalized the science fiction noir franchise about humanoid robots called “replicants” that slowly took over society back in 2017. The new series takes place 80 years after that original film. What’s changed? Who’s in charge now? Before you press play on Blade Runner 2099, let’s run through the cast and characters.

This is the official description of the series from Prime Video:

Which of the new characters are human? Which ones are replicants? Does it even matter anymore? Here’s what you need to know.
